[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.


Tool session rules:
- You have exactly one tool: `python`.
- When calling `python`, emit exactly the tool-call format shown elsewhere in this prompt for this model. Use only that format; do not add markdown fences, prose wrappers, or alternate tool-call syntax. Do not quote or place tool-call markup inside explanatory text; when you decide to call the tool, emit the tool call itself.
- Snippets are not saved, so re-import or redefine needed helpers. Use as many short, purposeful calls as needed to establish a clear probe or plan.
- `action(...)` refreshes runtime state immediately. Inspection-only calls preserve both `last_action_result` and `last_animation`.
- Each call has a 30-second limit and about 1024 output tokens. Keep output compact; truncation is reported.

# Ground on current_frame: understand the board, objects, and valid actions.
print("Level:", current_frame.level)
print("Step:", current_frame.step)
print("Shape:", current_frame.shape)

seg = current_frame.segmentation
nodes = seg['nodes']
adj = seg['adjacency_list']

print(f"\nTotal nodes: {len(nodes)}")

# Group nodes by color to identify key objects
from collections import defaultdict
by_color = defaultdict(list)
for n in nodes:
    by_color[n['color']].append(n)

for color, ns in sorted(by_color.items()):
    print(f"\nColor '{color}': {len(ns)} node(s)")
    for i, n in enumerate(ns):
        pixels = n['pixels']
        rows = [p[0] for p in pixels]
        cols = [p[1] for p in pixels]
        min_r, max_r = min(rows), max(rows)
        min_c, max_c = min(cols), max(cols)
        area = len(pixels)
        print(f"  Node {i}: area={area}, bbox=[{min_r},{min_c}]..[{max_r},{max_c}], hash={n['hash'][:8]}...")

# Check adjacency list for interesting connections
print(f"\nAdjacency pairs: {len(adj)}")
# Look for small/interesting adjacencies
for (a_id, b_id) in adj:
    a_node = next((n for n in nodes if n['id'] == a_id), None)
    b_node = next((n for n in nodes if n['id'] == b_id), None)
    if a_node and b_node:
        # Only print small or colorful adjacencies
        if a_node['color'] not in ['G', 'c', 'g'] or b_node['color'] not in ['G', 'c', 'g']:
            print(f"  Adj: {a_node['color']} (id={a_id}) <-> {b_node['color']} (id={b_id})")

# Valid actions
print(f"\nValid actions: {valid_actions}")

# Check last_action_result if available
if 'last_action_result' in globals() and last_action_result:
    print(f"\nLast action result: {last_action_result}")
</parameter>
</function>

# Ground on current_frame: understand the board, objects, and valid actions.
print("Level:", current_frame.level)
print("Step:", current_frame.step)
print("Shape:", current_frame.shape)

seg = current_frame.segmentation
nodes = seg['nodes']
adj = seg['adjacency_list']

print(f"\nTotal nodes: {len(nodes)}")

# Group nodes by color to identify key objects
from collections import defaultdict
by_color = defaultdict(list)
for n in nodes:
    by_color[n['color']].append(n)

for color, ns in sorted(by_color.items()):
    print(f"\nColor '{color}': {len(ns)} node(s)")
    for i, n in enumerate(ns):
        pixels = n['pixels']
        rows = [p[0] for p in pixels]
        cols = [p[1] for p in pixels]
        min_r, max_r = min(rows), max(rows)
        min_c, max_c = min(cols), max(cols)
        area = len(pixels)
        h = n['hash']
        if isinstance(h, int):
            h_str = str(h)
        else:
            h_str = str(h)[:8]
        print(f"  Node {i}: area={area}, bbox=[{min_r},{min_c}]..[{max_r},{max_c}], hash={h_str}")

# Check adjacency list for interesting connections
print(f"\nAdjacency pairs: {len(adj)}")
# Look for small/interesting adjacencies
for pair in adj:
    a_id, b_id = pair[0], pair[1]
    a_node = next((n for n in nodes if n['id'] == a_id), None)
    b_node = next((n for n in nodes if n['id'] == b_id), None)
    if a_node and b_node:
        # Only print small or colorful adjacencies
        if a_node['color'] not in ['G', 'c', 'g'] or b_node['color'] not in ['G', 'c', 'g']:
            print(f"  Adj: {a_node['color']} (id={a_id}) <-> {b_node['color']} (id={b_id})")

# Valid actions
print(f"\nValid actions: {valid_actions}")

<function=python>
<parameter=code>
# Ground on current_frame: understand the board, objects, and valid actions.
print("Level:", current_frame.level)
print("Step:", current_frame.step)
print("Shape:", current_frame.shape)

seg = current_frame.segmentation
nodes = seg['nodes']
adj = seg['adjacency_list']

print(f"\nTotal nodes: {len(nodes)}")

# Group nodes by color to identify key objects
from collections import defaultdict
by_color = defaultdict(list)
for n in nodes:
    by_color[n['color']].append(n)

for color, ns in sorted(by_color.items()):
    print(f"\nColor '{color}': {len(ns)} node(s)")
    for i, n in enumerate(ns):
        pixels = n['pixels']
        # Check type of pixels
        if isinstance(pixels, tuple) and len(pixels) == 2 and isinstance(pixels[0], int):
            # Single pixel as (row, col)
            rows = [pixels[0]]
            cols = [pixels[1]]
            area = 1
        elif isinstance(pixels, list):
            if len(pixels) > 0 and isinstance(pixels[0], (list, tuple)):
                rows = [p[0] for p in pixels]
                cols = [p[1] for p in pixels]
                area = len(pixels)
            else:
                # Flat list? Unlikely but handle
                rows = pixels[::2]
                cols = pixels[1::2]
                area = len(pixels) // 2
        else:
            print(f"  Node {i}: Unknown pixels format: {type(pixels)}")
            continue
        
        min_r, max_r = min(rows), max(rows)
        min_c, max_c = min(cols), max(cols)
        h = n['hash']
        if isinstance(h, int):
            h_str = str(h)
        else:
            h_str = str(h)[:8]
        print(f"  Node {i}: area={area}, bbox=[{min_r},{min_c}]..[{max_r},{max_c}], hash={h_str}")

# Valid actions
print(f"\nValid actions: {valid_actions}")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

# Check what changed after the UP action

# Compare previous and current frame
prev_frame = history[-2].frame
curr_frame = current_frame

prev_seg = prev_frame.segmentation
curr_seg = curr_frame.segmentation

prev_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in prev_seg['nodes']}
curr_nodes = {n['id']: n for n in curr_seg['nodes']}

print("\nNode changes:")
changed_nodes = []
for nid in set(prev_nodes.keys()) | set(curr_nodes.keys()):
    prev_n = prev_nodes.get(nid)
    curr_n = curr_nodes.get(nid)
    
    if prev_n and curr_n:
        if prev_n['boundary'] != curr_n['boundary']:
            changed_nodes.append((nid, curr_n['color'], prev_n['boundary'], curr_n['boundary']))
    elif not prev_n and curr_n:
        print(f"  Node {nid} ({curr_n['color']}): NEW")
    elif prev_n and not curr_n:
        print(f"  Node {nid} ({prev_n['color']}): REMOVED")

Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

# - The orange object is now near the top of the gray play area

    if n['color'] in ['O', 'b', 'B', 'W', 'w']:
        b = n['boundary']
        print(f"  {n['color']} (id={n['id']}): {b[0]}..{b[-1]}")

# Check adjacency to see if orange is touching anything interesting
adj = seg['adjacency_list']
o_nodes = [n for n in nodes if n['color'] == 'O']
if o_nodes:
    o_id = o_nodes[0]['id']
    print(f"\nObjects adjacent to orange (id={o_id}):")
    for pair in adj:
        if pair[0] == o_id or pair[1] == o_id:
            other_id = pair[1] if pair[0] == o_id else pair[0]
            other = next((n for n in nodes if n['id'] == other_id), None)

                print(f"  {other['color']} (id={other_id})")

Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.

[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.

# The orange object is stuck at [15,34]..[16,34] again. It seems to have a maximum upward position.
# Let me think about this differently.

# something else entirely. Let me look at the white cross object (W) inside the gray area.

# and then see what happens.

print("Trying DOWN repeatedly...")
for i in range(10):
    action(['DOWN'])
    
    o_nodes = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O']
    b_nodes = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'b' and n['id'] == 8]
    
    if o_nodes:
        print(f"Orange: {o_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{o_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
    if b_nodes:
        print(f"Blue 8:  {b_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{b_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")

[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

# The orange object is now at [45,19]..[46,19] and can't move LEFT further.
# Looking at the image, the orange is now on the left side of the gray area, near the white cross!

# Let me check the positions of the orange and white cross objects:
seg = current_frame.segmentation
o_nodes = [n for n in seg['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O']
w_nodes = [n for n in seg['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'W']

if o_nodes:
    print(f"Orange: {o_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{o_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
if w_nodes:
    print(f"White cross: {w_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{w_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")

# Check adjacency between orange and white cross
adj = seg['adjacency_list']
o_id = o_nodes[0]['id'] if o_nodes else None
w_id = w_nodes[0]['id'] if w_nodes else None

if o_id and w_id:
    are_adjacent = any((pair[0] == o_id and pair[1] == w_id) or (pair[0] == w_id and pair[1] == o_id) for pair in adj)
    print(f"Orange and White cross adjacent: {are_adjacent}")

# Maybe the goal is to get the orange to touch the white cross?
# Let me try moving UP to see if the orange can reach the white cross vertically.

print("\nTrying UP...")
action(['UP'])

o_nodes_after = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O']
if o_nodes_after:
    print(f"Orange after UP: {o_nodes_after[0]['boundary'][0]}..{o_nodes_after[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.

- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.

- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. # The orange moved UP to [40,19]..[41,19]. Let me continue moving UP to see if it can reach the white cross.

print("Continuing UP...")
for i in range(5):
    action(['UP'])
    
    o_nodes = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O']
    w_nodes = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'W']
    
    if o_nodes:
        print(f"Orange: {o_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{o_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
    if w_nodes:
        print(f"White cross: {w_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{w_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
    
    # Check adjacency
    adj = current_frame.segmentation['adjacency_list']
    o_id = o_nodes[0]['id'] if o_nodes else None
    w_id = w_nodes[0]['id'] if w_nodes else None
    
    if o_id and w_id:
        are_adjacent = any((pair[0] == o_id and pair[1] == w_id) or (pair[0] == w_id and pair[1] == o_id) for pair in adj)
        if are_adjacent:
            print("Orange and White cross are now ADJACENT!")
    
    if last_action_result.get('level_completed') or last_action_result.get('done'):
        print("Level completed!")
        break
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

# The orange moved RIGHT to [25,24]..[26,24]. Let me continue moving RIGHT to get closer to the white cross.

print("Continuing RIGHT...")
for i in range(3):
    action(['RIGHT'])
    
    o_nodes = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O']
    w_nodes = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'W']
    
    if o_nodes:
        print(f"Orange: {o_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{o_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
    if w_nodes:
        print(f"White cross: {w_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{w_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
    
    # Check adjacency
    adj = current_frame.segmentation['adjacency_list']
    o_id = o_nodes[0]['id'] if o_nodes else None
    w_id = w_nodes[0]['id'] if w_nodes else None
    
    if o_id and w_id:
        are_adjacent = any((pair[0] == o_id and pair[1] == w_id) or (pair[0] == w_id and pair[1] == o_id) for pair in adj)
        if are_adjacent:
            print("ADJACENT!")
    
    if last_action_result.get('level_completed') or last_action_result.get('done'):
        print("Level completed!")
        break

- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.

Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.

# The orange didn't move with DOWN. It's stuck at [45,39]..[46,39].
# Let me check the current state of all objects and think about what the goal might be.

seg = current_frame.segmentation
nodes = seg['nodes']

print("Current non-background objects:")
for n in sorted(nodes, key=lambda x: x['id']):
    if n['color'] not in ['c', 'C']:
        b = n['boundary']
        print(f"  id={n['id']:2d} color={n['color']} bbox={b[0]}..{b[-1]}")

# Check the yellow timer bar status
y_nodes = [n for n in nodes if n['color'] == 'Y']
r_nodes = [n for n in nodes if n['color'] == 'R']
print(f"\nYellow segments: {len(y_nodes)}")
print(f"Red segments: {len(r_nodes)}")

# Maybe the goal is related to the timer? Let me wait and see if the level completes when the timer runs out.
# Or maybe I need to do something specific before the timer runs out.

# Let me try moving LEFT to see if the orange can move from its current position.

[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Multimodal context:
- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.


Tool session rules:
- You have exactly one tool: `python`.
- When calling `python`, emit exactly the tool-call format shown elsewhere in this prompt for this model. Use only that format; do not add markdown fences, prose wrappers, or alternate tool-call syntax. Do not quote or place tool-call markup inside explanatory text; when you decide to call the tool, emit the tool call itself.
- Snippets are not saved, so re-import or redefine needed helpers. Use as many short, purposeful calls as needed to establish a clear probe or plan.
- `action(...)` refreshes runtime state immediately. Inspection-only calls preserve both `last_action_result` and `last_animation`.
- Each call has a 30-second limit and about 1024 output tokens. Keep output compact; truncation is reported.

# The orange moved LEFT from [45,39] to [45,34]. It's back at a familiar position.
# I notice the yellow bar is shrinking (only 1 segment now) and red segments are appearing.

# Let me think about this differently. Maybe the goal is to move the orange object to a specific
# target location within the gray play area. Looking at the image, there might be a target
# that the orange needs to reach.

# Let me try moving the orange to different positions and see if anything triggers level completion.
# First, let me try moving it UP to see if it reaches a target at the top.

print("Trying UP repeatedly...")
for i in range(10):
    action(['UP'])
    
    o_nodes = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O']
    if o_nodes:
        pos = o_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]
        print(f"Step {current_frame.step}: Orange at row {pos[0]}..{pos[1]}")
    
    if last_action_result.get('level_completed') or last_action_result.get('done'):
        print("Level completed!")
        break
    
    # Check if orange stopped moving
    if i > 0 and last_pos == pos:
        print("Orange stopped moving")
        break
    last_pos = pos
</parameter>

<parameter=code>
# The orange is stuck at row 15..34 again. Let me check the current state and think about what else to try.

seg = current_frame.segmentation
o_nodes = [n for n in seg['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O']
b_nodes = [n for n in seg['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'b' and n['id'] == 11]

if o_nodes:
    print(f"Orange: {o_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{o_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
if b_nodes:
    print(f"Blue node 11: {b_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{b_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")

# Check adjacency between orange and blue node 11
adj = seg['adjacency_list']
o_id = o_nodes[0]['id'] if o_nodes else None
b_id = b_nodes[0]['id'] if b_nodes else None

if o_id and b_id:
    are_adjacent = any((pair[0] == o_id and pair[1] == b_id) or (pair[0] == b_id and pair[1] == o_id) for pair in adj)
    print(f"Orange and Blue node 11 adjacent: {are_adjacent}")

# Maybe the goal is to get the orange and blue objects to touch each other?
# Or maybe I need to move them to a specific configuration?

# Let me try moving DOWN to see if the orange can reach the blue object below it.
print("\nTrying DOWN...")
action(['DOWN'])

o_nodes_after = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O']
b_nodes_after = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'b' and n['id'] == 11]

if o_nodes_after:
    print(f"Orange after DOWN: {o_nodes_after[0]['boundary'][0]}..{o_nodes_after[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
if b_nodes_after:
    print(f"Blue node 11 after DOWN: {b_nodes_after[0]['boundary'][0]}..{b_nodes_after[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. # The timer (Y=1, R=2) hasn't changed much. Let me check the current state more carefully.

seg = current_frame.segmentation
nodes = seg['nodes']

print("Current objects:")
for n in sorted(nodes, key=lambda x: x['id']):
    if n['color'] not in ['c', 'C']:
        b = n['boundary']
        print(f"  id={n['id']:2d} color={n['color']} bbox={b[0]}..{b[-1]}")

# Check if there are any new objects or changes
o_nodes = [n for n in nodes if n['color'] == 'O']
if o_nodes:
    print(f"\nOrange position: {o_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{o_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")

# Maybe I need to try a different approach. Let me see if moving the orange to a specific
# position relative to the white cross triggers something.

# First, let me move the orange to the same row as the white cross
w_nodes = [n for n in nodes if n['color'] == 'W']
if w_nodes:
    w_row = w_nodes[0]['boundary'][0][0]
    print(f"White cross row: {w_row}")

# The white cross is at row 31. Let me try to move the orange to that row.
# Currently the orange is stuck at row 15 when moving UP. Let me try moving DOWN first.

print("\nTrying to reach white cross row...")
# Move DOWN to get to a lower position where I can move horizontally
for i in range(5):
    action(['DOWN'])

if o_nodes:
    print(f"Orange after DOWN x5: {o_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{o_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
</parameter>

Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.

- User turns include an attached image of the current ARC grid.
- The image and `current_frame.ascii` are two representations of the same current frame.
- You can use images and other tools to understand the game state and guide your strategy, each may be useful depending on the current uncertainty.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Each call is a fresh snippet. Allowed imports: bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.

- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

# The orange is now at [40,34]..[41,34]. Let me try moving LEFT to get closer to the white cross column.

[TOOL CALL: python]
<tool_call>
<function=python>
<parameter=code>
# The orange didn't move LEFT from [40,34]. It seems like horizontal movement only works at certain vertical positions.

# Let me think about this differently. Looking at the image:
# - The gray play area has a specific shape with an indentation on the left side
# - The white cross is inside the gray area
# - The orange and blue objects are at the bottom-right of the gray area

# Maybe the goal is to move the orange object into the indentation or to a specific spot?
# Or maybe I need to trigger something by moving the orange in a specific pattern?

# Let me try moving UP first to get to a position where LEFT works, then move LEFT towards the white cross.

print("Trying UP to reach a position where LEFT works...")
for i in range(5):
    action(['UP'])
    
    o_nodes = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O']
    if o_nodes:
        pos = o_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]
        print(f"Step {current_frame.step}: Orange at {pos[0]}..{pos[1]}")
        
        # Try LEFT at each position
        action(['LEFT'])
        o_nodes_after = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O']
        if o_nodes_after and o_nodes_after[0]['boundary'][0] != pos:
            print(f"  -> LEFT worked! New position: {o_nodes_after[0]['boundary'][0]}..{o_nodes_after[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
            break
    
    if last_action_result.get('level_completed') or last_action_result.get('done'):
        print("Level completed!")
        break
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

# The orange is now at [25,29]..[26,29]. Let me continue moving LEFT to get closer to the white cross.

print("Continuing LEFT...")
for i in range(5):
    action(['LEFT'])
    
    o_nodes = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O']
    w_nodes = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'W']
    
    if o_nodes:
        print(f"Orange: {o_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{o_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
    if w_nodes:
        print(f"White cross: {w_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{w_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
    
    # Check adjacency
    adj = current_frame.segmentation['adjacency_list']
    o_id = o_nodes[0]['id'] if o_nodes else None
    w_id = w_nodes[0]['id'] if w_nodes else None
    
    if o_id and w_id:
        are_adjacent = any((pair[0] == o_id and pair[1] == w_id) or (pair[0] == w_id and pair[1] == o_id) for pair in adj)
        if are_adjacent:
            print("ADJACENT!")
    
    if last_action_result.get('level_completed') or last_action_result.get('done'):
        print("Level completed!")
        break

# The orange is now at [25,14]..[26,14] and can't move LEFT further. Looking at the image,
# the orange is now on the left side of the gray area, near the white cross!

# Let me check the current positions and see if they're adjacent or close.
seg = current_frame.segmentation
o_nodes = [n for n in seg['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O']
w_nodes = [n for n in seg['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'W']

if o_nodes:
    print(f"Orange: {o_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{o_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
if w_nodes:
    print(f"White cross: {w_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{w_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")

# Check adjacency
adj = seg['adjacency_list']
o_id = o_nodes[0]['id'] if o_nodes else None
w_id = w_nodes[0]['id'] if w_nodes else None

if o_id and w_id:
    are_adjacent = any((pair[0] == o_id and pair[1] == w_id) or (pair[0] == w_id and pair[1] == o_id) for pair in adj)
    print(f"Adjacent: {are_adjacent}")
    
    # Find what objects are adjacent to orange
    print("Objects adjacent to orange:")
    for pair in adj:
        if pair[0] == o_id or pair[1] == o_id:
            other_id = pair[1] if pair[0] == o_id else pair[0]
            other = next((n for n in seg['nodes'] if n['id'] == other_id), None)
            if other:
                print(f"  {other['color']} (id={other_id})")

# Maybe I need to move DOWN to get closer to the white cross vertically?
print("\nTrying DOWN...")
action(['DOWN'])

o_nodes_after = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O']
if o_nodes_after:
    print(f"Orange after DOWN: {o_nodes_after[0]['boundary'][0]}..{o_nodes_after[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

# The orange moved DOWN to [30,14]..[31,14]. It's now closer to the white cross at [31,21]..[32,21].
# Let me try moving RIGHT to get closer horizontally.

print("Trying RIGHT...")
action(['RIGHT'])

o_nodes = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'O']
w_nodes = [n for n in current_frame.segmentation['nodes'] if n['color'] == 'W']

if o_nodes:
    print(f"Orange after RIGHT: {o_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{o_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")
if w_nodes:
    print(f"White cross: {w_nodes[0]['boundary'][0]}..{w_nodes[0]['boundary'][-1]}")

# Check adjacency
adj = current_frame.segmentation['adjacency_list']
o_id = o_nodes[0]['id'] if o_nodes else None
w_id = w_nodes[0]['id'] if w_nodes else None

if o_id and w_id:
    are_adjacent = any((pair[0] == o_id and pair[1] == w_id) or (pair[0] == w_id and pair[1] == o_id) for pair in adj)
    print(f"Adjacent: {are_adjacent}")
